My banishment was sudden and unanimous, and I got whombo-combo’d out the door and out of the overhang we had stopped under by the entire crew. Even Zhuye and Suyan had been upset, my son and maid glaring daggers at me.

Washy plucked another piece of fish out of the jar, put it in his mouth, and with the concentration of a master sommelier, he chewed it, taking in every flavour.

“This is very mediocre fermented fish,” he observed.

It really was.

That was our road trip towards the Grand Ravine. We loved it.

We hated it.

We started planning the next one.

Chapter 642: V8C2: Arrival At The Ravine

I would say our journey was uneventful, but it certainly wasn’t. There was a certain kind of chaos only a family can bring; doubly so on a road trip. One second, we were about to murder each other; the next, back to being best friends.

And each step we took brought us ever closer to the Grand Ravine.

Originally, we had planned for the Grand Ravine to be one of our last stops… but due to the events of Ash Forest, the timetable got reshuffled a little. Fixing the northwestern Dragon Vein blowouts that turned into “Oh god, murder hornets are trying to eat the province” tends to throw a spanner in the works. We hadn’t known how long we were going to be then, so we just told the Lord Magistrate and Lady Wu to continue on to Grass Sea City without us.

After that mess had been dealt with, instead of zig-zagging southeast as we originally planned, we went there directly, skipping a bunch of stops to try and catch up to the original itinerary.. I made the executive decision to pull a kind of loop, heading to the Grand Ravine now—as it was closer—and then hitting the Verdant Blade Sect, the Misty Lake Sect, the Rumbling Earth Sect, and a couple other places before turning back and ending our trip at the sea.

While the landscape didn’t change too much as we approached, the rolling hills of the Grass Sea seemed to continue on forever, what did change were the people.

Or rather, the lack of change.

I had figured, with how big and powerful the Grand Ravine Sect was, that as we approached the Ravine itself the demographics would change. And they did, a little. At first individuals and pairs, then small groups of people who were wearing clothes more reminiscent of the Nezin tribe, but the numbers seemed rather small. As we chatted with them, we discovered that those outside the Ravine were mostly merchants, traders, or travelling workers, occasionally with their families.

But there seemed to be very few truly permanent residents. They seemed a little apart from the other people living here.

“They say the Ravine is their only real home,” one of the people we talked to said, an older gentleman whose face was so weatherbeaten it looked like old leather. He was wearing mostly Imperial clothes, but he also had a tattoo with a geometric design to it. “Which is true. But the real reason is if the bad times come again… they don’t lose too much when they retreat to the Ravine.”

“The ‘bad times’?” I asked.

“Sometimes, some fools get it into their heads that the barbarians don’t deserve this land and start causing trouble. Sometimes, it's a lot of fools. And sometimes it's a lot of fools with some sect or another footing the bill to get revenge on the Grand Ravine. It always ends the same way, lots of people hurt or dead for nothing,” he said before spitting to the side.

“How often does something like that happen?” I asked. The old man turned to look at me again. He looked from Yun and Gou to Biyu and Xianghua, then to our clothes.

“Ain’t been a real bad time since my grandfather was young. But there's been a few incidents… and old habits die hard,” he replied. “It’s been quiet enough recently, though. Can’t tell if it’s a good thing… or the calm before the storm. I hope it’s the first.”

Which… was certainly informative about the politics of the area around the Grand Ravine.

‘It's been so long, and it's back to how it was in the beginning,’ Tianlan whispered in the back of my head. She sounded resigned.

I sent a mental nudge. ‘Xiulan’ll get everyone back on the straight and narrow, don’t you worry.’

The Earth Spirit let out a snort of amusement at the mental image I sent her. “We just gotta let her work, hm?”

‘Yeah. If there's anyone who can bring people together… it's our Azure Heroes.’

I felt warmth flood our bond.

And so we kept going.

There was an incline now. Not very steep, but noticeable. We stopped ribbing each other so much as we got closer to the Ravine.

But then Tianlan put in a request.

‘Just a little further this way,’ she directed me, her voice thick with emotion. We were, from her memories, very close to the Grand Ravine… and she had a certain spot she wanted to see. A spot she remembered looking out over for the first time and truly seeing the Ravine with Xiaoshi, her first contractor.

I could feel the hesitation and apprehension in her voice.

‘You okay?’ I asked back.

Tianlan paused for a second before answering. ‘Even back then, the Ravine… it was so big. It felt like it would last forever. It was a part of me that I thought I would always have. But I thought the same about my mountains, my rivers, my geysers, and forests… and all of them were undone. I wonder if this is gone, too?’

We were quiet, as we continued up the incline… until we reached the top.

And the Grand Ravine opened up before us.

It was every inch as dramatic as the giant wall that was Yellow Rock Plateau at the southern edge of the province.

I had seen pictures of the Grand Canyon in the Before, the yawning chasm in the desert of Earth had been as grand as the name promised, but this put every single one of those images to shame. This titanic rent in the earth was nearly twenty kilometers across at this point—and I knew it was wider in other locations.

It wasn’t just a massive groove. The occasional spire of rock jutted up from the center, ancient worn stone nearly as high as our current elevation. A river that looked tiny from up here, but was actually at least a kilometer across itself, lazily snaked its way across the bottom of the Ravine, branching around the spires of rock, fed by more waterfalls that fell into the Ravine from rivers that emptied into it, creating a thousand rainbows.

But more than the rock and the water was the green.

The Ravine was verdant, the Grass Sea seeming to tumble down the sides of the Ravine and into its depths, grass and ferns and shrubs and water plants covering every surface. Great trees grew horizontally from the sides of the Ravine, their crowns curving upwards as they competed for light.

I immediately caught sight of a sloth lazily scratching its behind, before returning to its mission of eating leaves.

What at first I thought were odd, dark, fast-moving clouds turned out to be flocks of swallows, a thousand strong each, darting through the horizontal trees and rock spires without ceasing.

Condors the size of small airplanes glided on thermals, the scavengers calmly scanning the ground for their next meal.

Mountain goats plied the Ravine walls, hopping from tree to tree or simply doing that gravity-defying thing they did and running up bare stone.

Herds of deer and even bison travelled along the canyon floor, never wanting for any food.

It was an utterly majestic sight. All of us were struck dumb for a moment.

‘Ah,’ Tianlan murmured. ‘Even after everything… at least one thing managed to escape the worst of it.’

An image overlaid itself over my eyes. There was more erosion on the rock pillars. One of them had even fallen over.

And yet, out of everything… the Grand Ravine had changed little, this part of Tianlan managing to weather the storm, against all odds.

“What a beautiful view,” I whispered.

Naturally, we stopped and had a picnic, looking out over the grand vista before us. The wind blew through the valley; you could hear the distant roar of waterfalls, and the sounds of animals going about their lives. Bane and her girls dove into the Ravine, eagerly exploring and tasting the flowers that bloomed here.

It was lovely, under the warm summer sun, and provoked some rousing conversation.

“I’m pretty sure every canyon like this is made by water. Just the river flowing for thousands of years, and it does this,” I said, waving before us.

‘I have seen Brother Wa Shi’s water blades, so I know well the might of water—but even I could not comprehend the scale at which it could cut,’ Bei Be mused. ‘It is admirable. With time and persistence, even the least blade may cut such a grand sight.’

Sunny the plow rattled.

‘Yes, it could stand to be a bit cleaner, but it is water. You expect too much from something without any intent,’ the ox scolded, his voice amused. ‘And no, defacing the landscape just to prove we can make a cut this deep is impolite. Even if the cut is technically clean, the act itself would make it ragged.’

The plow ceased its rattling, and it seemed to be pouting.

“I’m also pretty sure the Grand Ravine Sect would be very unimpressed,” Yun Ren said, chuckling while he shook his head.

Summer’s Sky rattled as well with a questioning jangle, and Yun Ren laughed. “Yeah, they would probably refuse to serve us tea if they did that.”

I had a fairly good idea where Summer’s Sky stood on making new side passages in the Ravine. That sword was as obsessed with tea getting poured on it as Sunny was with cutting things.

“Though Brother Bei Be is correct. Seeing the power of a river like this is truly humbling,” Big D said, looking off into the distance. “Mortal, mundane water, making this—even in Xiaoshi’s time it was this vast and awe inspiring.”

“Aren't the canyons in the Howling Fang Mountains just as large?” Xianghua asked.

“They are, but the beauty is starker,” Rizzo said. “It’s a cold kind of beauty, where life clings at the fringes. This is… blooming.”

I let the various conversations wash over me, helping Meimei cook us lunch. Both of us would occasionally glance over at Zhuye… who was being very closely followed by Suyan as he toddled about because he kept glancing over at the Ravine, curiosity burning in his eyes.

He wanted to see off the edge.

I glanced at Mei, and then nodded at Zhuye. She smiled.

“I’ll finish up,” was all she said as I rose, scooping Zhuye up. I winked at Suyan… and then like a super responsible parent, I walked right to the edge of the several kilometers-deep Ravine.

Without being a cultivator, this would be sketchy as hell… but I would never fall unless I wanted to.

Zhuye gasped and started giggling, his eyes darting everywhere… until he saw a sloth.

He eagerly pointed at it.

“Dah!” he exclaimed, clearly wanting to meet the little beast… and well, I obliged him. I hopped down onto one of the trees.

I jumped down to the little fellow, who stared at us with eyes completely empty of all thought. The cute little guy even had what looked like a small, gormless smile on his face.

Zhuye giggled and immediately reached out a hand. I gently caught it. Zhuye looked up at me with shock and confusion.

“Don’t just touch, okay? We have to make sure he's a friend.”

I reached out my arm, and instead of touching, just held it in front of the sloth.

The sloth considered my arm at great length… then slowly reached over, clambering aboard.

“Gently, okay, little man? Gently,” I said to Zhuye. My son paused, the gears turning. He reached out again, gently, and stopped just before touching.

The sloth blinked at him. It sniffed at his hand... and then looked away, unconcerned. Zhuye carefully closed the distance, patting the sloth twice.

The sloth didn’t seem bothered. It was also a lot cleaner than the sloths I remembered from Before. There was barely any algae growing on him, and he didn’t smell bad at all.

I took him up with us, seeing as he was so unbothered, and we had lunch with him. Big D still had some fresh greens from home, from which he gave some to Zhuye so they could take turns feeding our new little friend.

I named him Sid.

The sloth was at first only mildly interested… at least until the first bite.

Then he couldn’t get enough of the stuff. The little traitor instantly abandoned me for Big D’s human form, and Zhuye followed instantly, the two of them sitting in his lap, Big D going full big brother mode.

It was very cute. Rizzo certainly thought so, watching with sparkles in her eyes.

Eventually, our lunch ended, and we prepared to set off. I set Sid back in his tree, and Zhuye waved goodbye to his new friend. Then we started back to one of the main roads so we could meet up with the Grand Ravine Sect and descend into the Ravine.

I snorted.

Meimei turned to me, curious about what I had found funny.

“We’re going to descend into Tianlan’s Grand Ravine,” I replied.

Meimei snorted. Tianlan groaned in the back of my head.

“It should be a very pleasurable journey for all of us, with how wet things are,” Meimei instantly replied, nodding at the waterfalls, and then we both started giggling like twelve year olds.

God damn I love this woman.

‘You two are idiots,’ Tianlan informed us, though I could hear her chuckling, too.

Chapter 643: V8C3: Herald Of The Earth

Ulagan Baatar, Patriarch of the Grand Ravine Sect, sat in peaceful repose upon the sect compound’s porch. Before him the training grounds stretched out, where the next generation of his people learned. It belonged to the youngest children today. They were going through forms or sparring with their fellows. Though the sparring inevitably dissolved into games and the forms into randomly flailing arms and legs as the children got tired.

Baatar smiled. He sipped his butter tea and looked around at the gleaming compound, everything freshly cleaned and prepared for their impending visitors.

The compound was somewhat of a compromise in location. It was here where the various tribes, clans, and families gathered in the distant past, to present the united front that was “The Grand Ravine Sect” to the outside world.

The defensive pact was made in darker times, when the survival of their people was at stake. When war was conducted openly, and the blood of their kinsmen and invaders both watered the Ravine in great torrents.

There the various tribes of the Ravine had sworn to set aside all differences and forget all enmity, in order to craft something that had lasted for thousands of years and had seen them through every crisis the children of Tianlan had faced.

It was one of the reasons why he had been comfortable declaring the same to the rest of the Azure Hills when Cai Xiulan had proposed the Azure Alliance. Blood feuds had been set aside once before in their history, and they had succeeded under the guidance of the Voice of the Earth.

With the Earth speaking once more, and so clearly… the path forward had been revealed. All who listened and allowed themselves to be brought onto the correct path, would prosper. Those who failed to heed the Earth would wither and fall, like rice planted in desolate soil.

In the time since their oath, obvious to all who had eyes and could see this fact, the children grew like climbing vines up the Ravine. Bottlenecks were finally broken, some of them having been stuck for nearly a century. Old places of power once more revealed themselves, allowing the children of Tianlan to once more see their ancestors’ wisdom and partake of the power they had left behind.

And who had they to thank for this glorious revival? As foretold by the Ancestors, the Earth awakened thanks to her Herald. They would be friend to all, and in their coming, all would be made whole again.

At first, they had been unsure if what had been foretold had indeed come to pass. Then they had been unsure if the Herald was Cai Xiulan or Master Rou.

But at the Dueling Peaks, Baatar had seen both… and he was sure. Cai Xiulan had some connection to the Earth, true. She was a mighty champion, chosen for some great destiny, but it was Master Rou who truly spoke with the Earth’s voice.

And now, he was coming to visit their Grand Ravine. Some of Baatar’s people, of course, were still unconvinced. Some still needed more proof, particularly those yet to meet Master Rou. Others were worried about their futures, with Master Rou being so clearly an Imperial, even if at least two of his disciples were kin to their northern cousins.

Baatar turned his gaze again to the children, who had abandoned any sense that they were supposed to be training. They were laughing and playing tag, their voices carefree and their hearts at peace.

He rather wished that his kinsmen could be more like these children.

Every Elder and Clan head was with Baatar on the porch or just inside the compound. Each and every one of them was tense like a strung bow, their unease palpable, waiting for the next report.

“You all need to learn greater patience,” Baatar scolded, breaking the quiet. “The hunter asks not when the herds come, nor the farmer asks when his crop is ready. It is ready when it is. It is the same with cultivators like Master Rou. He will arrive when he is ready.”

Several faces flushed. There were a few murmurs as the most powerful members of the Grand Ravine Sect accepted their chastisement… but most were still on edge.

Ah, how amusing were the youth?

And then, as if to underscore Baatar’s point, a messenger arrived, sinking to one knee before Baatar. “Reporting,” the messenger, Suld, declared. Everyone tensed, but Baatar knew it was nothing urgent.

“Excellent work. Come, have some refreshment before your report,” Baatar said with a smile… and driving home the point he was making. The others forced themselves to relax.

“Ah, thank you, Patriarch,” Suld said, accepting the tea his granddaughter, Sarnai, poured for him. She would normally be assisting the Grand Marshal at the Dueling Peaks, but she had been given leave to return for Master Rou’s visit.

After a few sips of tea, Suld was ready for his report. “Master Rou keeps his mild temperament. He allows us to observe his movements, and makes no move to dissuade us. We have confirmed the members of Master Rou’s party. Our cousins, the Xong Brothers, attend to him along with their spouses, Sect Master Liu Xianghua and Lady Cao Biyu; they are further joined by Fa Bi De and Hong Ri Zu.”

There were approving murmurs.

“He facilitates conversation between us and our cousins. That is good,” somebody said.

It was good. Especially when their cousins suddenly had at least two cultivators, who were in contention for “strongest cultivators in the province”, fall out of the sky and join them. Su Suyan and Xue Ji wore the colours of the north… and had mentioned having family in the south.

Things were quite interesting. Some would probably say he might be taking things too easily, but what could a man do, realistically, when the Earth moved? More powerful cultivators than they had lost against the whims of nature many a time. It was best to ride these upheavals out and adapt with them.

“Do you have a time for his arrival?” one of the still-impatient ones asked.

“We had a preliminary one… but we no longer think it is accurate,” Suld paused. He seemed uncomfortable, but continued. “He suddenly took a detour, and…”

All attention was on the man.

“And?” someone else prompted.

“We.. he… he is headed to Altan’s Outlook.”

At those words every murmur was silenced. Altan’s Outlook held a special place in the hearts of every true child of the Ravine. It was recorded that Honoured Ancestor Altan once said that that location was one that he enjoyed seeing with his dear friends, and that it had the best view of the Ravine. They strived to keep it as untouched as possible, so that all could see their home as their Ancestors did.

“You are certain?”

“Yes. He deliberately changed directions and is heading directly toward it.”

“Was he told?” someone else asked.

“No. Nobody mentioned it. No one from the Grand Ravine Sect has approached him, per our instructions. He just turned and headed directly towards the location. He's taking the right path, too, instead of the one that looks easier, but leads to a dead end… like he already knew it was there,” Suld reported.

Once more, there was silence.

And then Baatar laughed.

“Why are you so surprised the Earth’s Herald knows our land as if he was born here, walking in the footsteps of our Honoured Ancestors?” he demanded. “Were such things not made clear to us?”

He was met with silence.

“Master Rou is experiencing our home as it is meant to be experienced, witnessing the majesty of our Ravine from a spot Altan himself loved. This is a good thing. He shall be there for several hours hence; so let us adjourn this meeting and reconvene tomorrow. That is when he shall arrive.”

Baatar’s words were heeded and the Elders dispersed. Baatar himself turned back to watching the young ones. They were all gathered around one child, who was spinning a fanciful tale.

Baatar waited, his heart utterly calm.

And the next day, his prediction was met, for a dragon rose over the Grand Ravine, noble Lord Wa Shi, bringing word of his Master.

It was a little nerve wracking not to have the Lord Magistrate present to have one last conversation with before we stepped out of the carriage, but it was what it was.

Washy had reported back, and with that report came, thankfully, a warning.

A warning that there were a lot of people waiting for them.

As we got closer, Meimei’s nose started wrinkling as she scented the air, along with Zhuye.

“Huh. That's a lot of cultivators,” she said.

We stepped out of the carriage… And saw that, if anything, Washy was understating things. Our meeting point was a large town just on the edge of the Ravine, though calling it a town was false. It wasn’t a town, it was a fortress.

Geometric designs were carved into its walls, clean and well cared for… but there were small signs of damage. Anything big or compromising had been repaired, but this was a wall that had been tested.

It had a large gate, directly in its center, but the gate was unbarred and wide open.

On the field and around the town there had to be a hundred thousand people there to meet us. They came bedecked in colourful clothes and bore a riot of flags, a thousand colours fluttering on strings in the breeze—but one colour was most prominent.

Sky blue.

The field was covered in people, but it was rather orderly, with what were obviously civilians standing behind the lines of Grand Ravine cultivators. All of them were dressed similarly, with bandanas pulled low so they cast their eyes in shadow, and one half of their outer robes pulled down to their waist. It left half their body bare, with the women only having chest bindings, and it exposed the tattoos that went up their arms.

Some merely inked their hands, others had full sleeves, and some people’s tattoos that went all the way up onto their faces. There were a thousand different styles, but they had enough commonalities to see they had descended from a similar style.

Xiulan had said how big the Grand Ravine Sect was, but this was less that we were meeting the sect, and more that we were meeting the entirety of the Ravine Tribes… who had turned out to have far, far more people than the rest of the sects had previously realised.

The people closest to us were Baatar, and what had to be the rest of the Elders. They stepped forward.

“Master Rou, welcome to the Grand Ravine,” the man said, clasping his hands and bowing, along with the rest of them. But when I was about to bow back, Tianlan interrupted.

‘Take another step forward and hold your arms out, almost like you’re asking for a hug,’ she said in my mind, and I obeyed, trusting her. ‘It’s how you say hello in the Ravine!’

There were several sharp intakes of breath as I stepped forward and Baatar’s eyes widened, before a wide smile overtook his face.

He ceased his bow and stepped forward as well, taking my arms, his hands resting on my elbows.

“Amar mend üü?” I asked, repeating the words Tianlan told me. It meant something like “Are you well and peaceful?”

The old man’s hands shook for a moment, before he managed a nod.

“He knows the old ways!” someone whispered.

“Yes. Yes, we are well and peaceful, Herald of the Earth,” Baatar said, his smile full of... vindication?

I barely managed to keep my composure when he called me that. That was really on the nose.

“I am glad. I come to you under the old sky-blue banner, to see the sons and daughters of Chengis Altan—and see the beauty of the Grand Ravine,” I said, this part more rehearsed.

“And it shall be so. We greet you as friends. Let the blue sky shine over all of your endeavours—and may the Earth guide your path.” At that he released my arms and turned to the crowd. He raised one arm to the air. “My brothers and sisters! Let us welcome our friend! Uukhai!”

“Uukhai! Uukhai! Uukhai, Tianlan!” came the near-deafening roar. Horns blew, while hawks and condors screeched.

Like back at the Dueling Peaks, it was nearly overwhelming… and we started forward, through the gates, and down the slope into the Grand Ravine.

“Tianlan?” I asked. “Did you just make me Mua’dib myself?” I asked her.

“Mua—what?” she asked… and then I put down exactly what that meant to her. She paused. “Uh… I don’t think so?” she ventured… though she didn’t sound entirely sure. “In my defense, that's not some mystical old way stuff! Lots of people did it back when Altan was around! I saw people doing it in Pale Moon Mine! It's fine. It's fine! There's not gonna be any holy wars—probably.”

Probably?!

Chapter 644: V8C4: Into The Ravine

Thankfully, there weren’t any immediate shouts that I was the messiah and the Grand Ravine would be mobilizing to fight the Emperor for me, which would have been… awkward.

Instead, we were introduced to the Elders and people who were called “Clan Heads”. There were quite a lot of them, and I nodded along, trying to remember all of the Mongolian-sounding names. The Sectmaster also introduced us to his family; his son, Ganzorig, his wife, Oyuuma, and his grandchildren that were in attendance, which included Tarkhan and Sarnai.

‘None of them used their ‘shield names’, Tianlan said, sounding pleased. ‘If they introduced themselves with traditional names, that would mean they didn’t trust you.’

Next, we were guided through the fortress and onto a long sloping hill into the Ravine itself. The view was spectacular as we descended, the strata of rocks forming lines in the walls, half-covered by the horizontal trees that hung from them.

Horizontal trees that were covered with people. The group on top of the Ravine seemed to be only half the number of those who had come to see us. Entire families were perched on tree trunks, having picnics or just watching this strange man and his big carriage enter their home. Younger kids were tied to their parents by ropes around their waists, while older kids used those rope-dagger tools to make themselves swings, hanging underneath the tree trunks.

It looked really fun, especially when I caught a couple of kids basically going full Tarzan, swinging between trees.

It was actually a bit of a walk to hit the bottom of the Ravine, and when we arrived… I could really appreciate the place. I expected it to be hot and kind of stuffy, but instead a pleasant wind blew, tousling our hair.

“The air here is wonderful,” Bi De said, breathing deeply.

“It truly is,” I agreed, and Baatar and the other members of the Grand Ravine Sect seemed to stand up straighter. The sky above seemed wonderfully blue, and the greenery made everything look just a touch fantastical.

We soon arrived at the feasting grounds, where hundreds of what looked like yurts had been set up. Washy was already waiting for us. It all sat beside the river that ran through the valley, the waters crystal clear… and our gluttonous dragon was lounging like a prince while being attended to by what looked to be several chefs. Washy had a cup of tea in one hand, and had a bundle of herbs in the other. He sniffed it with great relish, nodding along to whatever the chefs were saying.

The herbs looked nothing like what we had, and I had a feeling that we were in for a treat... and some entertainment. What appeared like a jousting list was in front of the main table, but with archery targets, and there were what looked like canoes in the river.

Our seats were of course at the head table, which was arranged so we could see everything. The river, the list, and the trees above. Washy didn’t even have to move, being close enough to the table he could lean back slightly to see us.

“Tarkhan and Sarnai shall attend to you, Master Rou, Lady Hong,” Baatar said.

“I have heard only good things about them both,” I said, giving them both a smile. “You have my thanks for defending our daughter, Tarkhan, and Xiulan always speaks well of your assistance, Sarnai.”

Both of the cultivators puffed up at her praise. Tarkhan, whom we had known as Guo Daxian the Younger back then, had joined the fight immediately against the Shrouded Mountain Sect when he had heard Tigu had gotten kidnapped.

“You Honour me with your words, Master Rou. It was my honour to come to her defense,” Tarkhan said, pressing his fist over his heart. It was the arm with the tattoos. His bandanna was off his head as well, tied around his arm. “Tigu reached out her hand in friendship during the tournament. Only scum would strike such an earnest gesture away.”

Sarnai just went red. She did that a lot, from what I saw at the Dueling Peaks. She was a pretty nervous girl.

I nodded to them both and we sat back as the rest of the seats filled up. It again seemed to be more of a family affair; there were plenty of kids at the tables, and the mood was surprisingly light for what was effectively a state visit… but honestly I preferred it that way.

When the tables filled, Baatar rose, and a horn blew out. All conversation ceased.

“My brothers and sisters! Sons and daughters of the Grand Ravine! It has been too long since we have been able to gather in such numbers and share a meal with friends. Friends who greet us in the manner of our forefathers; who come under the banner of the true blue sky,” Baatar began, his voice echoing through the Ravine. “Today, the earth has so blessed us. Today, we welcome Master Rou, Lady Hong, their family, and our cousins from the north. Let us show our friends our warmest welcome!”

The answering cheer rattled the Ravine.

And then things truly began. It started with thirty Grand Ravine cultivators soaring over our heads, their rope-blades lashing out as they swung from tree to tree like Spider-Man, flipping into aerial acrobatics that made the people below cheer more. Eager kids started jumping up and down, raising their hands—and some of the Grand Ravine cultivators swooped down to grab said hands, taking the children up into the air as they shrieked with laughter.

I was immediately surprised at the instant inclusion of mortals into their routine, and the easy way the cultivators just grabbed them with big grins. In the other sects, even the Hermetic Iron Sect, there was a certain distance between cultivators and mortals that seemed to be less here. Baatar’s “brothers and sisters” apparently included even those without cultivation potential.

Which… I suppose if you had been fighting for thousands of years to keep your people and culture from being swallowed up, not having as big a gap between people was rather natural. A mortal had more in common with you than any cultivator from outside the Ravine.

And then the throat singing started. The whistling, reverberating vocalization bounced off the ravine’s walls, echoing into the distance.

I felt a smile form on my face as we watched the show.

And then the food started to come out. Back home, the Nezin tribe mostly ate what everybody else ate, save for the occasional bits of cheese; but here? It was undeniably stepping into another culture. Washy was practically giddy, clapping his hands together and wiggling in place as he took in the smells that had filled the air.

“Hmn. Man, this stuff smells amazing,” Gou Ren said.

“Our cousins know the good stuff when they smell it!” One of the Elders shouted, and that got several cheers.

Their bao were baked instead of steamed, filled with lamb and caraway. They had entire goats cooked with superheated river stones. There were stuffed tree marmots; the nearly twenty pound rodents that lived in the Ravine walls tasted great. There was horse meat and hand-pulled noodles, stews made of yak, and the thing every culture seemed to have, a roasted chicken. Gou, Yun, and Suyan really liked the meal; all three of them were very intent on eating.

All of it was hearty fare, meant to fuel you for long distance travel—and here you needed it. From one end of the Ravine to the other would have you walking at least three thousand Li; and that was taking the straightest path. It didn’t count the sixty thousand Li worth of side passages. The place was a maze.

They also, to my surprise, had absolutely titanic worms. The things could grow to about six feet long, and they were grilled like coiled sausages. I swear Big D’s eyes sparkled when he saw the things, and he absolutely demolished the one he had been given. He slurped it like an entirely too long spaghetti, and demanded seconds. This feat was apparently impressive, as he got several cheers.

There were also quite a few vegetarian options; they had something that almost tasted like curry, made with a kind of lentil that grew in the Ravine, and a bunch of stir-fried vegetables that were basically several varieties of bromeliad that clung on to the horizontal trees. Foraging in the Grand Ravine was certainly a vertical affair.

And then there was a slight hiccup. One of the things I had been looking forward to was seemingly missing.

“I heard the Grand Ravine had several fine cheeses,” I said, hopefully.

“We had been informed that the smell offends Lady Hong,” Baatar replied. “The plan was to serve it later, so we did not bring it close.”

Meiling glanced over. “It is fine. We are guests in your home, and compared to other things… cheese is not so offensive.” She gave me a little glare for that, my transgression with the fermented fish still not forgiven. “Mayhaps I have simply not had any good cheese?”

“Are you making fun of my cheese-making skill?” I demanded.

“Of course not, my dear husband,” Meiling replied, her voice all sugar. “Though the Muza Rela was quite good, and did not smell at all…”

Baatar laughed at the byplay… and then the cheese came out. Meiling’s nose mostly stayed unwrinkled, along with Zhuye. There was one kind that both of them eyed warily, and Zhuye hid his face from, which immediately disappeared… and I made a note to try it later.

The rest were great. It was interesting how the different milks changed the flavours; horse, goat, yak, and cow, each one was so different. Some were served with, to my surprise, a different kind of strawberry than the ones we had recently from Yellow Rock Plateau. These were tiny things, crushed into a paste on top of goat’s cheese. They had a cheese that was melted over their version of burgers; one that was grilled on sticks of wood—and then we hit the holy grail.

The absolute, most perfect, squeakiest cheese curds I had ever had the pleasure of biting into, that were already served in thick yak gravy.

They nearly had poutine.

“These… would go very well with fried potatoes,” I declared. “I must have the recipe for this gravy as well.”

“This one is quite good,” Meiling agreed, nodding.

And through it all, the show went on. There were races in river boats; horseback riding and archery contests. And like before, everything featured the normal people of the Ravine. There were beautiful musical instruments, prints, and wood work, as well as metalworking and jewelry. All of it had the same base, the geometric designs that made up their tattoos, but the way they wove things together was interesting.

“Oh, I have a pair that look just like these,” Meiling said, looking at a pair of earrings that had feathers attached to them. “Auntie Hu Li made them for me.”

“Indeed, Honoured Mother Hu Li made some for me as well!” Xianghua boasted.

“You name her Aunt?” one of the women we had been introduced to—Oyuun, Tarkhan’s mother, asked, her voice full of curiosity.

“Yes. She helped take care of me after my mother passed,” Meiling said with a fond expression.

“Mother-in-law is the best,” Biyu said, nodding along. “She’s very fun.”

“Aunt Hu Li is very kind,” Suyan even chimed in in that quiet voice of hers.

“She must be a powerful cultivator, to command such respect,” someone else mused.

“Mother Hu Li is a mortal,” Xianghua corrected, easily.

“Not like that would stop her from tanning our hides,” Yun Ren said.

“Don’t I know it,” Gou Ren grunted.

The table was filled again with laughter.

Baatar had a wide smile on his face as the feast progressed.

It was going splendidly. Master Rou was eating his fill with gusto. It was not the pecking of a man trying to be polite. Master Rou ate an entire plate of curds and gravy, then asked for seconds. His laugh was loud and boisterous.

The others, too, were clearly enjoying themselves, and the atmosphere was one of peace and camaraderie. He was surprised at how effortless it was. How easily their food was eaten, and how their art was praised. And not with empty platitudes, either.

It was genuine admiration.

Even if the topics were light for the moment.

And then, Master Rou paused. The feast was winding down, and slowly, he turned. He looked to the northwest, at the canyon wall.

He looked directly at the location of the most sacred place in the Ravine.

And then he turned back to his meal.

Baatar swallowed. For a moment, a flash of nervousness overcame him, before he pushed it aside. This was the Voice of the Earth, come to them as a friend. Of course he would know of the golden spire of rock that they had protected… wouldn’t he?

Chapter 645: V8C5: Seeing The World

Seeing the Grand Ravine was fascinating to Meiling; it was as interesting as when, earlier in the year, they had been visited by the Soaring Heaven’s Isle Sect.

She had always loved learning, and listening to just how differently people lived scratched an itch deep in her mind. But previously, she could only really listen. Read scrolls. Gaze at a drawing for an hour, or more recently, see images projected in front of her eyes.

Now? She actually got to be here herself! She got to see everything first hand, and it was amazing. The sights, the smells, the colours…

And the people.

The feast had turned into several groups of people talking to each other—Jin and Wa Shi were discussing cheesemaking with what had to be half the cheesemakers in the Ravine, her husband deep into the conversation. His eyes were sparkling as they always did when he was learning how to make new food, and Wa Shi was right beside him. At least most of these cheeses didn’t smell—even Zhuye didn’t mind them save for the particularly pungent ones.

Yun Ren and Biyu were, of course, showing off recorded images. Several of the women around them practically swooned as the image of Biyu’s arm, tied in red rope, showed up.

“It’s so simple and elegant—oh, I need to talk to Hangai—his clan lives so far away, but with this…!” one of the girls gushed. “What do you think, Elder?”

An old lady, who was wearing her hair in an elaborate head dress, squinted at the rope with suspicious eyes. She considered it at length, puffing her pipe.

“It keeps to the letter of our ways, but the spirit? Hmm,” she said in her wizened voice. “That it is the custom of our cousins is a mark in its favour, but it needs a proper discussion.” Several other older folk nodded seriously, but most just looked curious.

On the other side, Gou Ren and Xianghua were talking about hunting. Gou Ren was holding one of the Ravine’s bows, while a Ravine man was holding Gou’s, both were nodding at them, while Xianghua was stroking the feathers of a magnificent-looking hawk. The proud beast was headbutting her fingers, begging for more. With their headbands and clothes, Gou and Xianghua looked the most like they belonged here—Xianghua looked like a Ravine princess, if they had such things, with how her presence effortlessly dominated the attention around her. She laughed her booming laugh, and people laughed along with her.

“Earth and Sky, now that's a woman. Look how fierce she is,” she heard one of the men mutter.

“And how loud she can yell! No children will be able to say they can’t hear!” a woman joked.

Ravine ladies were supposed to be fierce and loud, and good at hunting apparently, which was interesting.

Bi De, Ri Zu, and Bei Be were all gathered around a selection of art and discussing its merits.

Suyan was still nearby, but was getting drawn into conversation herself, and there was at least one bee beside everyone.

And Meiling? She was satisfying her own curiosity. Zhuye was in her lap, dozing after the meal.

“And what did you say this was called?” Meiling asked, looking at the knives that were attached to a long rope.

“Olsokh Ir, Lady Hong,” came the prompt answer from Tarkhan. “Or an ‘Ascending Blade’ in Imperial. Every child, when they come of age, makes their own, and each family has their own styles. Long has my family been defenders of our home, so our blades are more suited to combat than purely used as a tool.”

She could see what he meant. His blade was long, hooked, and vicious-looking, sharpened to a razor’s edge. There were some parts of it that looked like the Nezin tribe’s blades, in the way it curved for hacking. The other example Tarkhan had brought up with him looked much more like a grappling hook. It had only a small cutting surface, while the rest was designed to be able to catch on to things and serve as an anchor point.

“Olsokh Ir,” she repeated, and Tarkhan blinked, before a small smile overcame his features.

“Your pronunciation is very good, My Lady.” He sounded impressed and surprised.

She had once been shocked that there was more than one language people spoke… but really, how could she have known? None of the scrolls she had read had ever mentioned humans speaking other languages, or if they did, it was always mentioned that it was far, far in the past, and when the Empire came everyone just started speaking the same language.

Everyone speaking the same language was something she had thought happened naturally. Of course everyone would want to communicate! What a good idea! Which… was probably a bit naive of her, thinking back on it.

The Nezin tribe spoke the Common tongue too. Occasionally, one of them would have a name that sounded a little different, and they had accents like Auntie Hu Li, but everyone could understand each other.

After the visit from Soaring Heaven’s Isle, she had even asked Auntie Hu Li and the tribe chief, Grandpa Han, if they knew any other languages. Auntie Hu Li hadn’t actually known, but Grandpa Han had just been amused.

“You’re using some of them,” he had said… and then pointed out several plants, three birds, what they called a specific kind of storm, and how they described light sparkling off rivers.

None of which were the “proper” names of things, but it was what her ancestors had recorded as the right names and what basically everyone in the Verdant Hill Commandery used. It was the Common tongue to them, and she would be the confusing one if she went anywhere else.

It was actually rather funny how little she knew; the realization was both humbling and exciting in equal measure.

“What about the hat, what is the hat called?” she asked, turning to Sarnai. She had a rather fetching red, round cap on her head.

The young woman brightened and began to explain.

Even after months in the province, Su Suyan could still marvel at how different things were in the Azure Hills.

“You came from the Howling Fang Mountains, cousin?” one of the Ravine folk asked, genuinely interested. Everyone here was looking with awe at her ears and her tail. Several children had their eyes locked completely on to the appendage, their fingers flexing like they wanted to run their hands through it.

How cute.

There was no hate in their eyes. No distrust. Just complete and utter fascination. The blue hair tie that she had been given by Chief Han seemed to be a magic talisman; every Ravine folk she had met who had noticed it had immediately become warmer to her.

It was odd that people who were not family were so eager to claim her as a cousin. Almost suspicious, if she was honest. They didn’t smell like the Nezin did, like home and safety. They were not truly kin.

But the earnestness of most of them were endearing. And they, more than most others… could understand the foxes of the Howling Fangs. They too had been fighting to preserve themselves for thousands of years. The circumstances were different, however.

They had, largely, won.

But there was a common thread there.

“Yes, we came following our Young Master,” she answered. “His family, and Master Jin’s, took us in. We have settled under his protection.”

There were noises of understanding, and then one of the cultivators spoke. One of what were the Ravine’s Elders, judging by her power… but most people here were weak. The only cultivator of note was the Patriarch.

“Perhaps we could arrange a visit, so we can better get to know our cousins!” the woman said. She looked in her thirties at most, her arms a riot of bangles, and her eyes sharp and calculating. “We can have some of our young ones build an outpost, so we can keep in touch better.”

The tone was slightly off. She recognised it instantly. The request was not entirely pure, and she knew why it was made. They lived near Master Jin. If the Ravine was allowed to visit, well, they might just run into Master Jin, or somebody might “get lost” and stumble onto his home.

The very thought of people disturbing the sanctuary that was her Master’s home was repulsive. Every instinct that had served Suyan in the years of hiding spasmed in her chest at the suggestion. Suyan’s soft smile died instantly. Instead, one eye opened, just so that the woman before her could see an amber iris… and the predatory slit of her pupil.

The woman’s good mood died as fast as Suyan’s had, the cultivator’s face going white as a sheet.

“That will not be happening,” Suyan snapped, her tone cold. Possibly too cold. Her Master wished for friendly relations with these people… but boundaries had to be set.

The woman swallowed and another of the Elders slapped the first on the shoulder, his glare scathing. This one was clearly older, his body that of a man well past his prime.

“She just got done telling us that they had to move into a different province, fool. We may be distantly related, but we’re still unknowns. Shall I go up to some Imperials and tell them where to find your house?” he demanded.

Instead of taking offense at her blunt denial, several of the Ravine folk were looking at her with sympathy, turning glares on the woman who had pushed, who let out a spluttered apology, bowing briefly, and practically fleeing.

“Wet behind the ears at that age. She's spent too long in the Ravine, she needs to see what the world truly is like,” the older man huffed. “Some people have more guts than sense. They see the might of Master Rou and they want to see more of it. It's like standing at the very edge of the Ravine, without checking if the ground is unstable. ‘One more step’, you say, then you fall.”

The conversational tone belied the warning that there would be others who wanted to politic about her Master. Suyan inclined her head at the warning; she would pass it on to her Master later, even if he probably knew already.

“That is an apt metaphor. We take our Master’s privacy very seriously,” Suyan gave her own warning, her tone turning mild again.

“If every one of his servants is as mighty as you, it's less stepping too close to the edge and more jumping down head first,” the older man snorted.

“As mighty as me? This Suyan is, at best, a middling talent,” Suyan denied. The old man’s eyes widened. “This one was chosen because she is good at attending to children without being too weak. Our actual combatants remain at home, guarding our Master’s land while he attends to matters here.”

“...I see.” the old gentleman said, his voice grave. “Well. it's a good thing there shall be no trouble, because nobody is foolish enough to disturb Master Rou.”

“Indeed,” Suyan said, taking a sip of her butter tea. Out of everything, this was perhaps the best invention of the Nezin and their distant relatives. The flavours seemed tailor made for the fox parts of her. “This is very good,” she said, raising her cup in salute.

The older gentleman took the out. “Thank you! It's our pride and joy. We build terraces into the side of the Ravine for the tea—we call it ‘monkey picked!’ Hoho!”

“We had something similar, up the sides of the mountains,” Suyan replied.

The boundaries were set; perhaps, they might loosen in the future, but for now… they needed to get to know each other first.

Night came early in the Ravine, the walls blocking out the sun, but the stars and the millions of fireflies more than made up for it, rising up over the river in great glowing clouds.

There were still demonstrations ongoing, and things had broken up further, the people of the Ravine singing and dancing.

We had exchanged gifts; my patented “farm produce” technique was as well-received as always, the entire crowd gasping as I laid out the food, drink, and Spiritual Herbs. I didn’t know much about their cultivation style, but they were the most numerous sect in the province… so I had settled on quantity rather than something more tailored.

Still, they clearly liked what I had given them.

In return, we had gotten things I had liked; art and fabric was nice; the Ascending Blades were cool as hell, but the crown jewel of what we received?

Rope.

Specifically, rope made by the Patriarch of the Ravine himself, and let me tell you, this stuff was absolutely insane! It was quite frankly the best rope I had ever held in this life. Every fiber was hand-selected by the Patriarch and woven with the expert precision of a man who had been doing it for hundreds of years.

And oh boy, I had so much use for rope. The drop hammer, Babe’s harnesses, my trestles for the climbing beans, fixing my fences…

Really, endless uses.

Even Tianlan was boasting about the rope quality from here. She was in a good mood all the feast, telling me to try this and that, and mentioning how much she liked other things. The food had changed a bit, but plenty of the flavours were still things she enjoyed.

And so I was in a really good mood when Baatar asked to speak with me, privately.

I had nothing better to do, so I followed him into the biggest yurt on the grounds here… where a bunch of other people were already seated and waiting for us.

I raised an eyebrow at what had to be all the Elders of the Sect, as well as people who were clearly mortal chieftains.

“Is this about what you said, back when we were atop the Ravine?” I asked.

“Yes, Master Rou,” Baatar said, his voice heavy. “We have long had a prophecy. A prophecy of a Herald of the Earth, who would one day return to us. They would know our customs as if born to them, and walk the earth, putting everything to right. We believe you are the Herald of the Earth.”

I blinked. I felt Tianlan blink.

Well, I mean, it wasn’t entirely wrong… that was honestly a pretty good prophecy. lt nailed what having Tianlan in your head looked like.

But there was one thing that was more important than this at the moment.

“Ha! Suck it, there is an ancient prophecy! You said there wouldn’t be one!” I shouted in my own head.

Tianlan spluttered. “How?!” she demanded, feeling absolutely baffled. “Wait, does this have to do with that Qi we felt?”

“Doesn’t matter how, what actually matters is how we defuse this! No holy wars allowed! No holy wars allowed!”

Chapter 646: V8C6: Herald

Baatar watched as his words landed. Master Rou’s face was always one of amusement or fascinated interest, and he seemed to always have an aura of friendliness around him. He had sat among them without a second thought, ready to continue their conversation.

There was always going to be a risk to telling an outsider of the secret core tenet of their beliefs. When the Imperials heard their words, to ‘Listen, for the Earth Speaks’, they imagined something less literal.

But every sign pointed to the man before them; and most of the Elders were in agreement. Some were still adamant that the Herald must come from the Ravine, that they must be one of their people. The people who had kept the faith, who had listened to the Earth. That some outsider was the Herald was like drinking bitter medicine.

And yet, there was precedent. Was not Ancestor Tianlan an outsider? Was not Ancestor Xiaoshi? The two righteous Ancestors who had joined Altan, fought against those that would kill the Ravine Folk, and adopted their customs?

It was a logic even the most reticent could not deny.

So their course was set.

Master Rou was told of their prophecy, and their eyes focused on the man before them.

Baatar and the rest of his kinsmen tensed as Master Rou froze… slight shock entered his eyes—and it was not the complete shock of a man who was told something world shaking. It was subtler. Muted. It was the look of shock that Baatar was more used to on his rivals and enemies. A shock that said “How did they figure this out?”

That, more than anything, was the final thing that should have removed all doubt from anyone watching.

And then his friendly smile faded. It was not anger, it was not hostility, but rather a more neutral expression.

That simple change shifted everything. Master Rou went from warmth and welcomeness and returned to being a mountain once more. Where before was a man who children approached without fear, who would listen intently to mortals with a smile, now there was the man who slaughtered thousands of demons, routed the Shrouded Mountain Sect with a letter, and who was proclaimed Master by the Cloudy Sword Sect.

And all that without the man touching a single drop of his Qi.

There were several swallows.

“That is indeed a heavy subject to spring on a man after such a wonderful feast,” Master Rou said, and some of the tension faded as he added a joke. “Let us say I am hypothetically this Herald of the Earth. What does the Grand Ravine demand of this Herald? And what does the Herald demand of the Grand Ravine?”

His eyes were focused solely on Baatar. Both were pertinent questions; and thankfully, Baatar had answers.

“It is only said that he will come as a friend and put the land to right. That he will usher in a golden era of peace and brotherhood; where our people will live without fear and walk all lands, not just the Grand Ravine; as they did in the time of Altan, Tianlan, and Xiaoshi,” Baatar said. “And we… are to assist the Herald however we can in making this a reality.”

Master Rou took a breath as he considered their words.

“And who was the one who gave you this prophecy?”

“The Earth told it to us, in the days near forgotten to memory; when heaven and earth were upended and all things broke. A golden spire, blazing with power and Qi, rose from the ground. A nameless ancestor came upon it and, in his greed, he sought to consume its power. But when he began to… he saw. He saw the past, he saw the present, and he saw the future. He saw what would happen if he consumed the spire, and he saw what would come again if he did not.”

Again, Master Rou looked in the direction of their most sacred and holy place… and then everyone felt it. Something vast beneath their feet had shifted its full attention. Master Rou looked at their assembly again, and there were gasps.

Master Rou’s eyes were normally the brightest, most vital green, flecked through with little dots of gold.

Now, those little flecks had grown. At first, they looked like jagged cracks, before the gold consumed his irises completely. The mountain of his Qi once more reminded them of its presence, spearing up and towering over the top of the ravine.

Something was resonating. The Qi changed, becoming more discernible to them all… and revealed only the unbridled truth.

The eager surge of gold felt like the pillar of amber every one of them had meditated beside. It was the exact same energy that suffused their sacred place. Its power swelled and grew, pushing toward their holy site—when Master Rou pressed his hand to the ground. “Calm down a sec, okay?” he muttered.

And just as quickly as the gold had come, it faded. Master Rou commanded the golden energy with the patient tone of a father gently rebuking an eager child. The power of the Earth, so vast and unfathomable, retreated back first into cracks, then little flecks captured by green life.

Master Rou closed his eyes, his shoulders hunching slightly, like he was considering something.

No. More like he was listening to something. A minute nod. A slight cock of his head.

The Earth was speaking to him, at this very moment. Everything was, without a sliver of doubt, true. Their ancestors, every man woman and child who had kept the faith, was, in this moment, absolutely vindicated.

No one dared to speak. To speak now and break the silence felt sacreligious. Baatar’s son and the previous Sect Master, Ganzorig, was sweating. Tarkhan looked like he wanted to throw himself down and prostrate himself. Sarnai’s eyes were wide and awed.

Master Rou opened his eyes. He considered them. And then he clearly came to a decision.

“Show us, please. We want to see this spire,” Master Rou, no, the Herald of the Earth spoke. His words were heavy, and his eyes were clear.

It was a blunt admission. He did not play any word games or try to hide it; nobody would have believed him if he did, but his strength would have enforced the lie.

He was respecting them enough to tell them the truth.

But there was one thing that stuck out.

“Us?” Baatar asked.

“You invoked her name earlier, Tianlan.” The revelation was almost too much. Great Ancestor Tianlan, who had deposed the Azure Emperor, who was one of the greatest friends to the Ravine… Master Rou could speak with her?! Was it her spirit? Was she tied to the earth after the great cataclysm? “She wants to see it.”

This opened up ten thousand more questions than answers, but all could feel the earth below their feet shifting. The rumble, the sheer vital energy beneath them.

“We will guide the Herald of the Earth,” Baatar said.

And so it was.

“Well… that went,” I mused to Tianlan, trying to inject some levity as we followed Baatar toward this spire… “Holy war is still up in the air, though.”

But Tianlan didn’t respond. Her Qi was bubbling and shifting, and I left her alone to her thoughts, save for the mental equivalent of a comforting hand on her shoulder.

I honestly wasn’t expecting the immediate excursion, and I thought we would at least rest and give them some time to discuss the idea if it was so important to them… but instead we left immediately. Those who were not cultivators were carried as we travelled down increasingly winding side passages… closer and closer to the resonance.

“They had a piece of me… and they just let it be?” Tianlan said, her voice fragile.

There was a lot of emotion in her words; everyone she had loved had ripped pieces from her in those dark days; torn her apart while she was still alive, and completed in shattering her already broken mind.

In a lot of ways, what the cultivators had done to Tianlan after the Breaking had completed what the demons had started. Maybe, if they had managed to set aside their differences, half-mad as they were, Tianlan could have recovered back then.

But they didn’t. And she hadn’t.

To hear that even after thousands of years of diminishing Qi an exposed part of her blood had been purposefully protected and preserved…

I could feel the joy, the gratitude, but also the fear of what she might find. It was enough that she had asked me to give them her name.

At cultivator speed, it didn’t take that long for us to get there, though we soon had to slow down. There were multiple hidden passages, murder holes, and guards that had to be spoken to as we went deeper and deeper into the Ravine, the walls closing over our heads. For a while, we were underground.

And then we hit a barrier. It was old and weak, but I could feel the ancient power behind its construction, holding on just enough. It flared for a moment, and then seemed to recognise us, fading once more into the background.

We stepped through, and the Ravine opened up again.

It opened up into something beautiful.

Moonlight poured into the glade, illuminating it with pure silver light. Fireflies danced through the air, and soft grasses and moss cushioned our feet. Flags, faded by time, were held up by rope, and three statues stood tall together, as if friends.

Two men and a woman. Their features had faded with time.

“Altan. Xiaoshi…” Tianlan whispered. I could feel her reaching out a hand, before she swallowed and then turned her attention to the center of the glade.

The crystal spire jutted from the ground slightly at an angle. It was about a foot and a half taller than I was, and as thick around as a large tree trunk. It gave off a dull amber radiance, it too worn by time, but it still stood.

And still emitted Qi. Tianlan’s Qi. Unbowed, and still there, even after so long. I felt it brush my face as we stepped into the glade. It brushed my face, and then paused.

Curiosity. Shock. Eagerness. Hope. It felt like Tianlan, so long ago, back when she was just a mass of emotion, rather than the person I now knew.

Tianlan’s own hands reached out… and the crystal started to glow. From amber to gold.

Which, naturally, is when we both got sucker-punched by the torrent of emotions and memories.

Chapter 647: V8C7: Pieces Of A Whole

The first thing that hammered into Tianlan’s consciousness was pain.

That, at least, was what she had been expecting. When she had reconnected to the shard of herself under the Dueling Peaks, it had been intense. There was so much history there, so many things had happened. The joy and the sorrow, the victories and the defeats; the place was steeped in memories.

But the Qi there had been weak. The echoes had been faint. It had been painful, it had been uncomfortable, but it had been nothing she couldn’t handle.

This shard of herself in the Grand Ravine was an order of magnitude more powerful, more aware. And so when she connected to it… it was vastly more intense.

Tianlan’s spirit realm shuddering was the first realization that something was going to be different.

Then the sky broke. The ground, run through with cracks of gold, screamed and fractured.

And the memory of her own breaking slammed into her. Tianlan felt her limbs shatter like cheap plaster, her teeth splintered in her mouth, and her vision exploded into fractals, her eyes cracked through like glass.

It hurt. It hurt so much. She thought she was tough. She had joked that pain was an old friend, and after what she had been through she could take anything the world could throw at her.

It turned out Tianlan was overestimating herself. Nobody could get used to their body being shredded into component pieces.

The panic made it worse. Her emotions were there too. The feeling of shock, of betrayal, of anger, of terror; the memories stabbed into her mind like knives, wrenching at the carefully constructed seams that held Tianlan all together.

And then a hand landed on her shoulder. In the middle of the maelstrom, a rock was suddenly there, holding her up, and taking some of the strain.

He was beside her. Not behind her, only helping the bare minimum. Not in front of it, taking the brunt of it. But beside her, as they had promised each other.

Jin was biting his lip so hard it bled. Every single muscle in his body was spasming. The noise that escaped from his clenched teeth was that of a great animal’s dying gasp. His pupils had dilated so much they had practically consumed the iris, and the seam in the middle of his body, the two halves that had fused into a whole, started to split and widen.

And yet he stood. His feet dug in, because he knew the only way out was through. Her partner. Her friend. Her Connected One.

And from further away, feeling the distress, another presence reacted. The golden stars in the dark sky flared, pushing back against the memories trying to rend Tianlan’s world apart, Meiling doing her best to lend her own helping hand.

The pain and emotions kept trying. Kept battering. From the tearing pain to the maddening, throbbing ache, as Tianlan was consumed. Her blood and bones were torn from the ground. Her people betraying her, consuming her, slaughtering each other—

But that, at least, was easier. She knew why they had done it. She knew why it had happened to them, happened to her; Tianlan had not been the only one hurt by her own destruction. The backlash had torn open her people’s very souls, driving half of them mad, and the other half delirious.

She had blamed them for thousands of years… when they weren’t fully to blame.

That thought resonated with the part of herself she had just reconnected to. The feelings sped up; the pain and terror as her awareness fell into the abyss, unravelling piece by piece, but the node here was strong. Too strong to fall asleep completely. It had just enough power to feel. To feel the betrayal.

And as the memories surged again, to know terror.

She saw a man approach the crystal. He was a child of the Ravine, his power in the Spiritual Realm. She saw the shock, then the delight, then the greed in the Ravine Man’s eyes as he approached. As he began to consume.

The shard of Tianlan threw its memories at the man, barely a step above animalistic instinct—but compared to what Tianlan experienced, it was a hollow echo, forced against his soul, yet even a fragment of that power was potent enough.

The Ravine Man spasmed and staggered, his joy and glee turning to shock and horror as he felt for himself Tianlan’s break. How he felt the betrayal. How he felt the power draining out of him one bit at a time.

With this much power on the line, however, she knew that such feelings mattered little to most. All the Ravine Man would have to do was brush aside the feelings, and claim the might for himself.

Yet this child of the Ravine did stop. Guilt, remorse, and horror filled his soul at what he had been about to do.

The shard of Tianlan had been shocked—shocked, but then hopeful. Happy. Altan had been there since nearly the beginning in their war against the Azure Emperor. The sons and daughters of the Ravine had been some of their strongest supporters, taking in refugees, caring for Spirit Beasts, and providing as many soldiers as they could spare for what had been their army. Altan had always been reliable… and so, too, it seemed, was this descendant.

Her shard took pity on the reeling man, trying to soothe his panicked thoughts. It showed him the good times. The times Tianlan had loved, swinging through the Ravine, laughing with Xiaoshi and Altan. Building the Dueling Peaks together with everyone; the feeling of connection she had, with Xiaoshi, but with the Ravine, too. And for a moment, the shard felt hope. That maybe, it could be like that again.

The shard was rather confused when the man took, “I hope that it can be like that again” to mean “It will be like that again.”

Tianlan felt her own thoughts jolt. The entire ‘prophecy’ was caused by a misunderstanding?

The man was overjoyed at what he saw. She felt his resolve firm, his goal for the future suddenly set. The man pulled away from Tianlan’s blood, and bowed deeply, before disappearing.

Then, in the blink of an eye to her shard, reappearing, with more people. Once more the shard felt fear.

But no one approached. No one touched. Altan’s descendants bowed their heads, in gratitude and reverence.

They believed in the man’s words. They believed in that misunderstanding. They gasped as the shard of Tianlan reached out curiously to them, but their souls were too far away, too distant. They had vague feelings, nothing more… but those vague feelings were enough.

They sat with her. They meditated near her. They loved her, they revered her, they called her a fragment of a goddess; and in her they trusted absolutely.

The memories sped up. The pain dulled. The darkness encroached on the edges of the shard’s vision. It was constantly on the edge falling into the abyss, its Qi draining away over thousands of years. The shard went from glowing brilliant gold to a dull amber.

And still people gathered around it. They sang songs. Every year they danced.

And that staved off the final oblivion.

The shard, though its vision was dark around the edges, saw a hundred generations grow up around it. It tasted the echo of their dreams. It tasted the feeling of their reverence. It shuddered under the force of their worry and despair, when once more they had to go to war.

Through it all, the shard felt the hope. The hope, that it was said, one day, the Herald of the Earth would return.

One day, things would be better. And not even just solely for them, the children of the Ravine. Most believed the Herald would spread their gifts to all… and they were content with that.

They just had to last. They just had to keep defending their home. The Ravine was enough for their mighty ancestors, so the Ravine was enough for them.

The vision faded. Jin heaved in a shuddering breath. “God damn,” he muttered, his voice a rasp. Tianlan reached up and placed her hand over his. No words needed to be said. He knew her gratitude, just as she knew his. The stars above twinkled gold—and they could hear grumbling about poisoning what had just hurt her and her Connected One. Tianlan giggled at the fierce protectiveness coming from Meiling, and let out a breath, looking back down.

The link fully connected, solidifying, and provided power to the old, ancient pillar of her blood.

The amber spire that had appeared in her mindscape glowed, going from dull to blinding gold like a sunrise, and from that light, something moved.

Before them… stood herself.

Or a Tianlan that was not quite Tianlan. There were differences, disjoints, and things familiar but not quite.

She was older than her current form. She stood slightly taller than Tigu, slightly shorter than Xiulan. She had freckles on her nose, and eyes of the purest sky blue. Her bearing was regal, a queen, a goddess. She was the earth and the sky, dressed entirely in the clothes of the Ravine, of blues vibrant and bold. On her hand was a tattoo that disappeared up her sleeve, on her waist was an Olsokh Ir, and on her back was a bow.

The shard of Tianlan raised her hands up so she could see them, looking fascinated. She touched her face, and tears sprung to the corners of her eyes.

“I’m… me, again?” she asked, her voice wavering, like she couldn’t believe it, but even wavering it was regal, bold, and beautiful.

Tianlan knew the feeling all too well. She had felt the same disbelief when she had first woken up. The same wonder.

Beside her, Jin bent down. His hand touched the earth, and with it, went his Qi. Her other part jolted. Her eyes snapped to them. Two blue eyes landed on her and her Connected One. Ancient. Measuring. Weighing.

Jin stood under that weight, under the assessment… and smiled at her shard, his Qi radiating only honesty and warmth.

“Hey there, sleepy head,” he said. “Take your time. You’ve been asleep for quite a while.”

“It's rough, waking up so suddenly,” Tianlan agreed, smirking at the older version of her, more like how she had looked when she had incarnated into a human body, so long ago.

Her other part’s eyes softened. The tears at the edges of her eyes beaded and began to fall. She took a tentative step forward, then another, then another, stumbling, almost falling, when Jin and Tianlan caught her.

She held onto them like they were about to disappear; and Tianlan shared her own memories, sending them down the thickening link much more gently than her shard had given her memories.

Because damn, Tianlan still had a bit of a headache! That had hurt!

Chapter 648: V8C8: Differences

Being awake again was so strange. She could think. She could feel. She could remember.

It was all so overwhelming, and the only reason why she didn’t just curl up and weep for the next century were the others.

They had gathered her up, and taken her to a warm, smooth stone under the sun, overlooking the representation of the Grand Ravine in her domain. They held her, gave her time to think, to sort through some of the chaos and the sensations that were flowing through her mind.

The view was beautiful. Calming. It filled her with awe and wonder and pride, to look upon the Grand Ravine, to know its beauty. She saw it, through a thousand different eyes, and a thousand different pieces of art.

Thank Tianlan. Praise Tianlan. Ah, Tianlan blesses us with this beauty.

She was Tianlan. Or… part of Tianlan, she supposed. The other part utterly dwarfed her, as large as the Grand Ravine was. The shard of Tianlan could feel the echoes of steam, snow, stone, iron and marble, a massive lake, the Dueling Peaks, the expanse of the Grass Sea and the lingering smoke from Ash Forest.

The Ravine was beautiful. But all those other places had their own beauty, too. A farm in the north, so wonderful and full of life. An Iron Dragon, roaring and carrying people to their destinations. Hot springs rising out of the earth, their cousins laughing and drinking with Imperials without fear, the thunder of water down the Gutter.

Spires of rust and metal, gleaming and reaching for the heavens; a perfectly circular lake. Creations of an upheaval the Ravine had largely been spared. Beauty, made from destruction. The surge of light and life, a river of emotion, compared to the quiet solitude that had been her life in the Ravine.

She looked to the side. Somewhat paradoxically, she looked better than the greater part of herself. Her eyes found the Tianlan who had the form of a young girl. Tianlan had entire swathes of her body clad in gold, cracks running through her body, and her clothes were just slightly frayed at the edges.

She knew it was the differences in circumstances. She, the shard of the greater whole, had been cared for and tended to. There were a few small cracks at her wrists and her neck, but the care the Grand Ravine had for her corporeal form was obvious.

The other part of her had not been treated so kindly, torn to pieces and bleeding out—and yet her heart still had room for love. She could feel that through the thickening connection between them. Her larger whole had her own fears and reservations, and yet still, even after everything, she could feel joy and happiness.

Most of it was due to the man beside her. Her contractor. Their contractor. She could feel the bond. She could feel the rightness. Not just with him, but from another presence, who would glance over in concern.

He was steady and enduring, like the earth itself. He cared. He cared enough to willingly come from his life away from politics and cultivators because the earth was wounded and hurting.

He had come to make things better.

“To think that a prophecy founded on a misunderstanding… ended up coming true anyway,” she muttered to herself, echoing the sentiment that she could feel in her connection.

“I know, right?” her larger, yet shorter portion, asked, her grin cheeky.

“It's funny how things end up working out, huh, Tianlan?” her contractor asked, before his brow furrowed. “Hmmm. Calling you both Tianlan is gonna be a bit confusing. How about Xiaolan and Gaolan?”

Small-Lan and Tall-Lan.

“Really, all that to make a joke about our heights?” her shorter part scoffed, while she felt some amusement at the nickname.

“If that is what you will, mine contractor, I shall bear it with pride, like a shield name of the Ravine,” Gaolan said, inclining her head. The movements came easily. Powerful. Regal. That was what the Ravine thought of her, and thus it was so. She had a thousand thoughts, actions, etiquette floating around in her mind.

Her contractor seemed taken aback at her tone of voice, and the bow, before turning to her smaller, yet larger half.

“Look at how regal she is. Now that's an Earth Spirit. You need to take some lessons on gravitas, huh?” he teased.

“Haaa?!” came the immediate eruption from Tianlan. “I can do gravitas! You’ve seen me do gravitas!”

“You throw mud and headbutt people,” her contractor rebutted.

“Both are valid responses to what I have to deal with! It's a precision technique, just ask Xiulan and Tigu! They both were cured by percussive maintenance!” Tianllan said proudly.

“I’m pretty sure both of them just call you an annoying gremlin…”

The two of them began squabbling like children, sniping and jabbing and getting heated on the outside, while their souls bore nothing but amusement and happiness. Her smaller half ended up tackling their contractor, and their foreheads connected with the sound of rock on rock—

And underneath all that, was the hope that their little show was amusing to her.

Indeed, it was. Despite how things had changed, she and her bigger half still had the same sense of humour. The headbutts, the poking and prodding… it was like being with Xiaoshi again.

Even though that thought provoked a dull ache in her chest, Gaolan found herself chuckling at the two rolling around in the dirt.

They were both utter fools, but being so bright and open was how people were supposed to act among friends and family. Better to burn hot and have a fist fight then keep it to yourself and stew for years, eroding the bonds between kin.

That was how the Ravine did it! Or was supposed to do it.

It was strange, to know what she ought to be. To finally have form to truly be what the people of the Ravine thought she was—Tianlan, the great Ancestor who had protected them, who continued to protect them.

The goddess whose name was on their lips when they marched to war.

Gaolan frowned at the thought. It sat uneasily with her. She was ancient… and yet most of that time had been spent as a mass of instinct and emotion.

She reached for her connection to the greater whole, to help her make sense of these new strange feelings. She pressed towards her larger half, hoping to see more. It felt right, to press forward. To immerse herself in herself, to become one, to return to what was right—

A hand on her shoulder snapped her out of the thoughts, gently pushing her away, and back to the surface.

“Hey. Hey, you still with us?” Tianlan asked, her voice worried.

Gaolan gasped, as she realised what had just happened… and that provoked a fresh round of confusion.

“Why… did you not just merge with me?” The question was quiet. She was part of Tianlan, after all. Why had her other part allowed her to awaken? Why had she held off, and allowed her to exist, when by all rights, she should have just been returned to the whole?

“Because you’re different enough to choose,” came the calm, matter of fact answer. “At your core, you’re me. But the years have made you into something else. There's a core to you that’s… separate. We could have just merged, but I want you to have a choice… and I wanted to get to know the lady that Altan’s descendants took care of so well.”

“Choose?” Gaolan asked. Nobody had ever asked her to choose before… she simply had been.
