“Maybe it's better this way. Maybe it's worse. I’m not sure yet. But what I do know… is that you deserve to see the Grand Ravine, as more than a mass of instinct. Whatever happens next… we’ll figure it out! Stay separate or join back together, it's all good with me!” Tianlan said cheerfully, and held out her hand.

It was rather an absurd thing to say. If she wanted to stay separate, she could? Part of her was baffled by the offer. She was but a small shard of what was Tianlan.

And yet another part of her, the part the Ravine Folk loved and worshipped… appreciated it.

Gaolan took the offered hand.

Baatar stared anxiously as the crystal glowed brighter and brighter. The Qi of the earth filled the air and filled this most sacred, holy place.

The Herald of the Earth’s eyes had been closed for perhaps half of an hour, when everything suddenly shifted.

The earth trembled. The crystal glowed. And for an instant, a woman made of golden light appeared. Her dress was that of the Ravine; her eyes were Tianlan blue.

She placed a hand on the Herald’s shoulder, a soft smile coming to her lips. Then, the woman looked upon the gathered Elders, her vast presence pressing against them.

Thank you. For everything.

The voice was a whisper on the wind; and yet it touched Baatar’s soul.

And then she was gone.

The Herald of the Earth rose, and the crystal’s light dimmed slightly, but it maintained its inner light; gold now, instead of amber. Revitalized. There was no sudden outpouring of great strength. And yet… they could feel the difference in the air.

“You’ve taken good care of this,” the Herald said, turning to face them. He considered them all. He weighed them. He clearly was having another internal conversation with the Earth, his head tilting.

“Your prophecy is at least partially correct,” he finally spoke. “This land is broken. The Dragon Veins were shattered, long ago—before the battle on the solstice weakened them even more. The entire point of my journey through these Hills is to repair them.”

Baatar made to sink to his knees. They all did, but the Herald raised a hand. His green eyes bored into them all.

“That will not be happening,” he stated bluntly. “I will take no oaths of loyalty, nor will I take a leading role in anything that you think of. You will not be prostrating yourselves, nor declaring my superiority to others—that is, if you have any respect for my wishes. If you do not… by all means. But I will not be pleased.”

His words were the coldest any had heard from the man. The crystal beside him glowed brighter for a moment, as in emphasis.

Everybody wisely stayed standing.

“I came here to fulfill a promise to a very good friend, and fix the Dragon Veins. As for the rest of your prophecy, why would I be needed? It's already happening. This golden era, where you can walk without fear… aren’t you already walking down that path?” the man asked.

Baatar closed his eyes as he realised what the Herald was getting at. It was the same reason why Cai Xiulan did not march into the Dueling Peaks as an Empress. Their orders would be followed. But would the people’s hearts be moved, when a command was given, instead of the bonds forged between people grown naturally?

“Are you not already working with Cai Xiulan? Were you not the first to declare that you would strike out old grudges? Do not all the sects now work together to repair what your ancestors built?”

Truly, this man was worthy of being the Herald of the Earth. His vision was long and clear.

Baatar had scolded the others for having no patience—but here he had been hoping the man before him could simply snap his fingers and make their dream a reality.

Of course things were not that easy. And yet he was correct. They had already started down the path they desired.

“It is as you say, Herald of the Earth,” Baatar replied.

The man nodded.

“Now, I think that's enough excitement for one night,” he said with a smile. “I’m looking forward to seeing the sunrise from the Ravine tomorrow.”

Chapter 649: V8C9: Small Steps

The walk back to where we would be staying for the night was silent. None of the Ravine folk seemed to want to break the quiet, and honestly, it suited me fine right now… because I was pretty fried.

I had managed to suck it up for Tall-lan’s sake, but god damn that had hurt. I wanted to curl up and whimper for a while. Since arriving into this world, I had been beaten to death, hit with multiple lightning bolts, cut up, smashed though multiple trees and a mountain, been poisoned—yet half of what Tianlan had felt was probably the worst pain I’d ever been in.

I was managing to keep how I felt about the entire experience under wraps from Tall-Lan, but Tianlan knew. I could feel a mental touch, equivalent to a hand on my shoulder.

The feeling of gratitude was enough to lower the boil of my emotions into a dull simmer. I had promised my friend. I had followed through with it. Yeah, it sucked… but the memory of the pain would fade.

Of course, I’d do it again without a second thought.

But I was still simmering, and right now I knew I was fixing to bite somebody’s head off. I had already been a bit snappish at the Ravine Folk earlier. Hells, I probably would have started smacking bitches if any of the Ravine Elders had pushed the issue, but luckily for them they were smart enough to actually listen.

Thank Tianlan, I suppose. No wonder she was so fond of these people, they actually had brains—or at least knew when a fight wasn’t going to go their way.

Maybe it was Tianlan’s feelings infecting me. The memories of Altan were quite vivid. I would have liked to have met the guy. He was vivacious, full of life, and absolutely ride or die. The Ravine Folk had always been Tianlan’s friends. Whenever she had been sad, or feeling lonely, she had come here, to bask in the Ravine, or to simply see her friend.

Tianlan trusted these people enough to let them know at least a bit about herself. We’d see how it all panned out in the future. But for the moment, playing the mysterious Master and saying they were already on the right path seemed like the correct choice. I’d like to think I was getting better at acting. It was getting easier, at least… and the general grumpiness I was feeling had probably helped a little.

We got back to the campgrounds pretty quickly, and I was shown to our quarters for the night; a large yurt. Big D was on top of it. He raised a brow and I nodded at the rooster, indicating that I was fine. He hopped down to my shoulder. Rizzo popped out from under his coat, sniffing at me.

I bid the Ravine Elders good night, a bit more pointedly than I probably would have normally. Again, they got the hint, bowing and dispersing.

I let out a little sigh and entered the yurt. I wasn’t too out of it to notice how nice it was. It wasn’t as grand as the Magisterial Palace in Grass Sea City, but it had something those cavernous rooms didn’t. A kind of warmth. The windows were open, letting the cool breeze of the Ravine blow through the structure—and what a wonderful breeze it was, full of the smell of flowers and greenery. The walls were lined with rugs, showcasing the designs of the Ravine. They were really nice. Thick and plush, and I would have to get a few of these. There was a stove and a chimney as well, both currently unlit, and the bed was large, with one of the stove’s pipes going under the bed to warm it in winter.

I could see myself actually living in one of these; maybe I should make one, because this was practically glamping.

Meiling was standing in the center of the room. She had been reading a scroll, and looked up when I came in. Zhuye was already asleep. My wife stood without comment and marched up to me, her purple eyes searching mine.

“Are you alright?” Meiling asked, her voice quiet. Her fingers ghosted over my body, glowing a soft green. She was searching for aches and pains, but there were none. It was all in my head in the end.

“I’m good,” I replied, catching her fingers. “Are you?”

“I only caught a bit of it,” Meimei said, waving things off. “Though even that much was… bracing.”

I felt Tianlan’s wince, and Meimei gave her a soothing mental pat.

“Bed,” I declared, reaching down behind Mei’s legs and scooping her up. Meimei just smiled.

Zhuye stirred as I got into bed with my wife, clambering over to lay on top of me. Big D settled onto the pillow where my neck met my shoulder, his feathers warm and soft, while Rizzo disappeared into Meiling’s sleeve.

I let out a sigh. Family cuddles made everything better.

I fell asleep and didn’t dream.

The morning had me feeling a lot better and ready for the day ahead. It was really interesting seeing the Ravine break down the yurts and the campgrounds. They were just so fast at it.

“Breaking camp swiftly is something everyone learns,” Baatar had informed me when I asked, and I caught the implication. In case they needed to run and disappear into the side passages.

The Patriarch hadn’t changed after last night. He seemed to have taken my words to heart, acting exactly the same as before, which was appreciated.

The others looked to be trying as well, though several of them made aborted bows when they saw me.

A work in progress.

Our breakfast was a kind of soup—meat, fat, and berries that grew in the Ravine. It was simple fare, but I had asked for what they would normally eat instead of a grand feast like last night. It was filling and energizing.

It was good, and with the tea, we were soon ready for the first thing we were going to do today—Olsokh Ir practise.

I was actually excited I got to be Tarzan. The rope blade was surprisingly intuitive… and doubly so when I had Tall-lan in my head helping me through things. Though we did need a different name for her, Tall-lan was fine as a joke, but… something for the future.

I ended up kind of speeding through the beginning portions of the explanation, mostly because I was cheating.

“And then around the forearm like that,” Tall-Lan said, keeping it tight… but loose enough to slip my arm out just in case. Her voice was firm, but full of warmth. It was a little strange to have a second passenger, but she was enough like Tianlan. I didn't really notice it.

I let it rip, swinging the grappling hook-like tool to wrap around one of the tree’s branches, then kicking off and swinging to another horizontal tree. With a jerk of the rope, it loosened from the bind it had on the branch, which allowed me to pull it back in while I landed. I couldn’t help the grin that came to my face.

“You’re a natural, Master Rou!” Tarkhan called.

I looked down from the top of the tree and smiled, sitting down for a moment to watch the others. Rizzo had also taken to it like a natural and swiftly ascended to be beside me. Gou Ren and Xianghua came next, the two of them both surprisingly good at it, while Biyu was taking a recording of Yun Ren hanging upside down. Meimei missed her first throw and laughed while Sarnai helped correct her form. Big D was the furthest behind—mostly because he and Babe were still deciding on which kind of knife head he should use on the end of his rope.

Washy had appointed himself “lifeguard” in charge of catching anybody who fell. He was relying entirely on the bees to pay attention for him, because he was utterly consumed with whatever he had on the stove… and it wasn’t like any of us could actually get hurt by falling at this point.

Suyan was sitting with Zhuye, who was playing with a length of rope, looking at it curiously.

The people of the Ravine who had stayed were all looking on with small smiles, and Baatar seemed particularly pleased, the Patriarch of the Grand Ravine seated on a rock.

The old man had three kids using him as a jungle gym, and he didn’t seem to care about the propriety of the matter, simply looking utterly at peace.

…they really did do things different in the Ravine. And they were making it really easy to like them. I turned from the others, and looked at the side of the Ravine.

“Xiaoshi could make it to the top of the Ravine in less than ten seconds,” Tall-Lan goaded. Tianlan and I laughed at the blatant challenge

So I took her up on it, my Olsokh Ir unlimbering. My eyes roved over the trees, planning a path.

And then, I was Tarzan. Or Spider-Man. But hopefully not George of the Jungle; I don’t think the trees would survive me hitting them.

“Whooo!” I cheered.

“Not bad!” Tall-Lan declared.

Tall-Lan’s personality was more reserved because she had been in shock. With the morning, and time to recover? She was definitely still Tianlan.

Even after thousands of years receiving what was basically worship, her core was the same. She was a bit different. More… regal, I guess, and like I had joked last night, she spoke with gravitas. But she was hardly reserved. She was actually kind of loud. She was less excitable than Tianlan, less animated, but just as relentless.

Which honestly made sense, considering what the Ravine said was a proper woman.

“Let's go!” Tianlan cheered as I swung twice around a tree before launching myself nearly vertical.

Which thinking about it… might have been based off of Tianlan in the first place?

Everything kinda fit. Fierce, unafraid to speak her mind, fond of hunting and fishing and climbing. And above all, a defender of the home.

Had Tianlan, while she was incarnated, accidentally made an entire culture think that tomboys were the ideal woman?

That would actually be kind of hilarious.

Chuckling to myself, I pushed the thought from my mind and instead focused, swinging up the Ravine wall until I was at the top of it.

I was, naturally, slower than Xiaoshi and Altan, but it was still fun as hell. I crouched on a tree near the top, just taking it all in.

I let out a breath, and let the breeze flow through my hair.

“Ah… I’ve felt this a thousand times in my memories. But here… it feels so different,” Tall-Lan whispered.

“It’s always different in person,” I agreed. I stood up from my crouch on the tree, and let out another breath. I allowed myself to tip backwards… and I fell.

The wind whistled past my ears, the horizontal trees flashed by me, with little tilts of my shoulders adjusting my drop.

And at the last moment, my blade lashed out. It wound around a tree, and I swung around it, bleeding off momentum, before releasing it and landing at the bottom of the Ravine once more. The rope uncoiled from my arm with ease, and I looped it into my belt and sheathed the blade in one motion that felt right. Whoops and cheers from the watching Ravinemen resounded, echoed by Zhuye.

I think I was going to enjoy myself for the rest of this visit.
